Configuration Poisson groupoids of flags
Jiang-Hua Lu, Victor Mouquin, Shizhuo Yu

Abstract
. Let G G be a connected complex semi-simple Lie group and ℬ {\mathcal{B}} its flag variety. For every positive integer n n , we introduce a Poisson groupoid over ℬ n {\mathcal{B}}^{n} , called the n n th total configuration Poisson groupoid of flags of G G , which contains a family of Poisson sub-groupoids whose total spaces are generalized double Bruhat cells and bases generalized Schubert cells in ℬ n {\mathcal{B}}^{n} . Certain symplectic leaves of these Poisson sub-groupoids are then shown to be symplectic groupoids over generalized Schubert cells. We also give explicit descriptions of symplectic leaves in three series of Poisson varieties associated to G G .



1.
Introduction and statements of results

1.1.
Introduction

Symplectic groupoids, and more generally Poisson groupoids, were introduced by M. Karasev
[
14
]
and
A. Weinstein
[
27
,
28
]
to study singular
foliations and quantizations of Poisson manifolds.
A Poisson manifold
is said to be integrable if it is the base of a symplectic groupoid.
While not every Poisson manifold is
integrable (see
[
4
]
for the obstructions), “natural” Poisson manifolds are expected to have natural integrations to symplectic groupoids.
When the Poisson manifold is algebraic, one would also want the symplectic groupoids to be algebraic.
It is a fundamental problem of Poisson geometry to construct explicit and systematic examples of symplectic groupoids, especially in the category of
algebraic Poisson manifolds.

Lie theory provides a rich class of Poisson manifolds:
every connected complex semi-simple Lie group
G
carries a
standard
multiplicative Poisson structure
\pi_{\rm st}
,
defined using the choice of a pair
(B,B_{-})
of opposite Borel subgroups of
G
(see
\lx@sectionsign
3.1
), and many important manifolds in Lie theory
carry Poisson structures closely related to the Poisson Lie group
(G,\pi_{\rm st})
. Four series of such Poisson manifolds have been introduced and studied
by the first two authors in
[
17
,
18
]
. Among them are the two series of quotients of
G^{n}
, denoted respectively as

\widetilde{F}_{n}=G\times_{B}\times\cdots\times_{B}G\hskip 14.45377pt\mbox{and}\hskip 14.45377ptF_{n}=\widetilde{F}_{n}/B=G\times_{B}\times\cdots\times_{B}G/B,\hskip 14.45377ptn\geq 1.

Here and for the rest of the paper, we consider the right action of
G^{n}
on itself by

(g_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{n})\cdot(h_{1},h_{2},\ldots,h_{n})=(g_{1}h_{1},h_{1}^{-1}g_{2}h_{2},\ldots,h_{n-1}^{-1}g_{n}h_{n}),\;\;h_{j},g_{j}\in G.

(1.1)

Then
\widetilde{F}_{n}
is the quotient of
G^{n}
by
B^{n-1}\times\{e\}\subset G^{n}
, while
F_{n}
is the quotient of
G^{n}
by
B^{n}\subset G^{n}
.
By
[
17
,
\lx@sectionsign
7.1]
, the product Poisson structure
(\pi_{\rm st})^{n}
on
G^{n}

projects to well-defined Poisson structures on
\widetilde{F}_{n}
and on
F_{n}
,
respectively denoted as
\widetilde{\pi}_{n}
and
\pi_{n}
. See
\lx@sectionsign
2.2
for a more general construction.

For
Z=\widetilde{F}_{n}
or
F_{n}
, denote by
[g_{1},\ldots,g_{n}]_{\scriptscriptstyle Z}
the image in
Z
of

(g_{1},\ldots,g_{n})\in G^{n}
. Let
T=B\cap B_{-}
, a maximal torus of
G
. Then
T
acts on
\widetilde{F}_{n}
and
F_{n}
respectively by

\displaystyle t\cdot[g_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{n}}=[tg_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{n}},

(1.2)

\displaystyle t\cdot[g_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{F}}_{n}}=[tg_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{F}}_{n}},

(1.3)

preserving the Poisson structures
\widetilde{\pi}_{n}
and
\pi_{n}
. A systematic study of the
T
-orbits of symplectic leaves, or
T
-leaves
for short (see
Definition
1.3
), of both
(\widetilde{F}_{n},\widetilde{\pi}_{n})
and
(F_{n},\pi_{n})
are given in
[
18
]
.
In particular, it is shown in
[
18
, Theorem 1.3 and Theorem 1.1]
that both
(\widetilde{F}_{n},\widetilde{\pi}_{n})
and
(F_{n},\pi_{n})
have finitely many
T
-leaves.
Setting
{\mathcal{B}}=G/B
, the flag variety of
G
, note that for each
n
one has the isomorphism

F_{n}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{B}}^{n},\;\;[g_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{n}}\longmapsto({g_{1}}_{\cdot}B,\,(g_{1}g_{2})_{\cdot}B,\,\ldots,\,(g_{1}g_{2}\cdots g_{n})_{\cdot}B).

We thus also regard
F_{n}
as a product of flag varieties. Similarly, one has the isomorphism

\widetilde{F}_{n}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{B}}^{n-1}\times G,\;\;[g_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{F}}_{n}}\longmapsto({g_{1}}_{\cdot}B,\,(g_{1}g_{2})_{\cdot}B,\,\ldots,\,(g_{1}g_{2}\cdots g_{n-1})_{\cdot}B,\,g_{1}g_{2}\cdots g_{n}).

As the main results of the paper, we show that for each
n\geq 1
the Poisson manifold
(\widetilde{F}_{2n},\widetilde{\pi}_{2n})
is a Poisson groupoid
over
(F_{n},\pi_{n})
, and that certain
T
-leaves of
(\widetilde{F}_{2n},\widetilde{\pi}_{2n})
(resp. symplectic leaves therein) are Poisson (resp. symplectic) sub-groupoids.
We also give several isomorphic models for the Poisson groupoids, which shed different lights and put them in different contexts.
The isomorphic models are established through
Poisson isomorphisms between
T
-leaves in various
T
-Poisson varieties, the proofs of which, being technical, are presented in the appendices.
In Appendix
\lx@sectionsign
D
in particular, we determine symplectic leaves in all the
T
-leaves of three series of
T
-Poisson varieties
including
(\widetilde{F}_{n},\widetilde{\pi}_{n})

for all
n
. We remark that while
T
-leaves of many
T
-Poisson varieties associated to the Poisson Lie group
(G,\pi_{\rm st})
have been
determined (see, for example,
[
7
,
15
,
18
]
), describing the symplectic leaves therein is a harder problem
and it has been done only for the case of
(G,\pi_{\rm st})
itself by M. Kogan and A. Zelevinsky in
[
13
]
.
Results in Appendix
\lx@sectionsign
D
thus constitute a big step towards a general theory of
leaves in
T
-leaves

and is thus of independent interest.

In the rest of the Introduction, we explain our motivation and give more details of the main results of the paper. See in particular
\lx@sectionsign
1.3

on identifications of the total and base spaces of the Poisson groupoids in this paper with cluster varieties studied by L. Shen and D. Weng
in
[
25
]
, and with augmentation varieties of Legendrian links by H. Gao, L. Shen and D. Weng in
[
9
]
.
We also point out the recent work

[
1
]
by D. Alvarez, which contains a construction of a Poisson groupoid over
F_{n}
as the moduli space of flat
G
-bundles over the disk with
decorated boundary. Precise relations between the Poisson groupoids in
[
1
]
and the ones in this paper, and further study on their
dual Poisson groupoids and double symplectic groupoids will be given elsewhere.

1.2.
Generalized Schubert cells and configuration Poisson groupoids of flags

Let
W
be the Weyl group of
(G,T)
, and recall that the flag variety

{\mathcal{B}}=G/B
has the decomposition into Schubert cells
1
1

1

In the literature,
BuB/B
, for
u\in W
, is sometimes
called a Bruhat cell in
G/B
.
In this paper, we use the term Schubert cell, reserving the term
Bruhat cell
for the sub-manifold
BuB
in
G
as suggested by A. Berenstein.

{\mathcal{O}}^{u}:=BuB/B
, where
u\in W
. Similarly, for
n\geq 1
and
{\bf u}=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{n})\in W^{n}
, denote the image of

Bu_{1}B\times\cdots\times Bu_{n}B
in
F_{n}
as

{\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u}=(Bu_{1}B)\times_{B}\cdots\times_{B}(Bu_{n}B)/B\subset F_{n}.

One has the disjoint union decomposition

F_{n}=\bigsqcup_{{\bf u}\in W^{n}}{\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u}.

For
{\bf u}\in W^{n}
,
{\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u}\subset F_{n}
is called a
generalized Bruhat cell
in
[
5
,
18
]
. In this paper, we will
refer to them as
generalized Schubert cells
to be consistent with the case when
n=1
.
By
[
18
, Theorem 1.1]
, each
generalized Schubert cell
{\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u}\subset F_{n}
, being a union of (finitely many)
T
-leaves of
\pi_{n}
,
is a Poisson sub-manifold of
(F_{n},\pi_{n})
.
Following
[
5
,
18
]
, the
restriction of
\pi_{n}
to
{\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u}
, again denoted as
\pi_{n}
, is called the
standard Poisson structure
on
{\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u}
.

In this paper, we first give in
\lx@sectionsign
2

a general construction of a series of Poisson groupoids associated to any Poisson Lie group and a closed Poisson Lie sub-group.
The natural Poisson groupoid structure on
(\widetilde{F}_{2n},\widetilde{\pi}_{2n})

over
(F_{n},\pi_{n})
is then a special case of the general construction (see Theorem
3.2
). We introduce the sub-manifold

\Gamma_{2n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\rm def}}}{{=}}\{[g_{1},g_{2},\ldots,,g_{2n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{F}}_{2n}}:g_{1}g_{2}\cdots g_{2n}\in B_{-}\}

of
\widetilde{F}_{2n}
, which, by Proposition
3.1
and Theorem
3.2
, is a union of
T
-leaves of
(\widetilde{F}_{2n},\widetilde{\pi}_{2n})
and a Poisson sub-groupoid of
the Poisson groupoid
(\widetilde{F}_{2n},\widetilde{\pi}_{2n})\rightrightarrows(F_{n},\pi_{n})
. We call

\theta_{\pm}:\;(\Gamma_{2n},\widetilde{\pi}_{2n})\rightrightarrows(F_{n},\pi_{n}),\hskip 14.45377ptn\geq 1,

the
n
th total configuration Poisson groupoid of flags of
G
or simply the
a total configuration Poisson groupoid
, where

\theta_{+}
and
\theta_{-}
are the source and target maps.
For each
{\bf u}\in W^{n}
, let

\Gamma^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}\theta_{+}^{-1}({\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u})\cap\theta_{-}^{-1}({\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u})\rightrightarrows{\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u}

be the
full sub-groupoid
of
\Gamma_{2n}\rightrightarrows F_{n}
over
{\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u}
(see Definition
1.1
) . We show in Theorem
4.4
that

\Gamma^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})}
is a single
T
-leaf of
(\Gamma_{2n},\widetilde{\pi}_{2n})
. Consequently

\theta_{+},\theta_{-}:\;\;(\Gamma^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})},\widetilde{\pi}_{2n})\rightrightarrows({\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u},\pi_{n})

(1.4)

is an (algebraic) Poisson sub-groupoid of
(\Gamma_{2n},\widetilde{\pi}_{2n})\rightrightarrows(F_{n},\pi_{n})
. Furthermore,
we show that
the symplectic leaf of

(\Gamma^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})},\widetilde{\pi}_{2n})
through the section of units of the groupoid in (
1.4
) is an (algebraic) symplectic groupoid over
({\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u},\pi_{n})
.
We call
(\Gamma^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})},\widetilde{\pi}_{2n})\rightrightarrows({\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u},\pi_{n})
a
special configuration Poisson groupoid of flags of
G
.

Our interest in generalized Schubert cells
{\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u}
with their standard Poisson structures
\pi_{n}
, for

{\bf u}\in W^{n}
, stems from some of their remarkable features
in relation to cluster algebras and to other Poisson manifolds related to the Poisson Lie group
(G,\pi_{\rm st})
.

First of all, it is shown in
[
5
]
that for any
{\bf u}=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{n})\in W^{n}
, one can use root subgroups of
G
and
reduced decompositions for each
u_{i}
to
parametrize
{\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u}
by
{\mathbb{C}}^{l({\bf u})}
, thus obtaining the so-called

Bott-Samelson coordinates

(z_{1},\ldots,z_{l({\bf u})})
on
{\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u}
and a Poisson bracket
\{\,,\,\}_{\bf u}
on

{\mathbb{C}}[z_{1},z_{2},\ldots,z_{l({\bf u})}]
.
Here
l({\bf u})=l(u_{1})+\cdots+l(u_{n})
and
l(u_{i})
is the length of
u_{i}
.
Explicit formulas for
\{\,,\,\}_{\bf u}
are given in
[
5
, Theorem 5.15]
in terms of root strings and structure
constants of the Lie algebra of
G
.
In particular, it is shown in
[
5
]
that the polynomial Poisson algebra

({\mathbb{C}}[z_{1},z_{2},\ldots,z_{l({\bf u})}],\{\,,\,\}_{\bf u})
is a
symmetric Poisson CGL extension
in the sense of K. Goodearl and M. Yakimov,
a special class of Poisson polynomial algebras
introduced and studied in
[
11
,
12
]
by the same authors in the context of cluster algebras.

Secondly, generalized Schubert cells with the standard Poisson structures
form basic building blocks for
many of the Poisson manifolds associated to the Poisson Lie group
(G,\pi_{\rm st})
.
For example, it is shown in
[
20
]
that a number of Poisson homogeneous spaces
(G/Q,\pi_{{\scriptscriptstyle G}/{\scriptscriptstyle Q}})
of
the Poisson Lie group
(G,\pi_{\rm st})
,
including
(G,\pi_{\rm st})
itself and
(G/B,\pi_{1})
,
admit so-called
Bott-Samelson atlases
which are built out of generalized Schubert cells, and the Poisson structure
\pi_{{\scriptscriptstyle G}/{\scriptscriptstyle Q}}

is presented as symmetric Poisson CGL extensions in all of the coordinate charts of the Bott-Samelson atlas.
We refer to
[
20
]
for more detail.

The explicit and natural Poisson and symplectic groupoids over the generalized Schubert cells
({\mathcal{O}}^{u},\pi_{n})

constructed in this paper add another dimension to
this distinguished class of Poisson manifolds. The Poisson groupoids
(\Gamma^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})},\widetilde{\pi}_{2n})\!\rightrightarrows\!({\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u},\pi_{n})

are interesting on their own. Indeed,
we give three additional isomorphic models of
(\Gamma^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})},\widetilde{\pi}_{2n})\!\rightrightarrows\!({\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u},\pi_{n})
, each having
advantages over the others and putting
these Poisson and symplectic groupoids in different perspectives.
We now give more details on these models.

1.3.
Two isomorphic models of
(\Gamma^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})},\widetilde{\pi}_{2n})\rightrightarrows({\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u},\pi_{n})

Let
{\mathcal{B}}=G/B
be the flag variety of
G
, and let
{\mathcal{A}}=G/N
be the

decorated flag variety
(also known as the basic affine space) of
G
, where

N
be the unipotent sub-group of
B
. Let
{\mathcal{A}}^{o}=B_{-}N/N
, an open subvariety of
{\mathcal{A}}
. For
n\geq 1
, set

{\mathcal{C}}_{2n}={\mathcal{B}}^{2n-1}\times{\mathcal{A}}^{o}
.
Under natural isomorphisms
{\mathcal{C}}^{2n}\to\Gamma_{2n}
and
F_{n}\to{\mathcal{B}}^{n}
,
the Poisson groupoid

(\Gamma_{2n},\widetilde{\pi}_{2n})\rightrightarrows(F_{n},\pi_{n})
becomes the Poisson groupoid

({\mathcal{C}}_{2n},\,\widehat{\pi}_{2n})\rightrightarrows({\mathcal{B}}^{n},\,\overline{\pi}_{n}),

(1.5)

see Theorem
3.4
for detail.
Correspondingly, for each
{\bf u}\in W^{n}
, the special configuration Poisson groupoid

(\Gamma^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})},\widetilde{\pi}_{2n})\rightrightarrows({\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u},\pi_{n})
is then isomorphic to a Poisson sub-groupoid

({\mathcal{C}}^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})},\,\widehat{\pi}_{2n})\rightrightarrows({\mathcal{B}}^{\bf u},\overline{\pi}_{n})

of the Poisson groupoid in (
1.5
), where

{\bf u}^{-1}=(u_{n}^{-1},\ldots,u_{2}^{-1},u_{1}^{-1})\in W^{n}
if
{\bf u}=(u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{n})
(see Corollary
4.5
).
The variety
{\mathcal{C}}^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})}\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{2n}
consists of sequences of flags with relative Tits distances prescribed by
{\bf u}
(see Lemma
4.2
)
and is an example of a
decorated double Bott-Samelson cell
introduced by L. Shen and D. Weng in

[
25
]
.
We explain in
\lx@sectionsign
A.2
that
({\mathcal{B}}^{n},\overline{\pi}_{n})
is a
mixed product
of
n
copies of the Poisson variety
({\mathcal{B}},\pi_{1})
in the sense defined in

[
17
]
, and a similar statement holds for the Poisson variety
({\mathcal{C}}_{2n},\widehat{\pi}_{2n})
.
By
[
25
]
, each
{\mathcal{C}}^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})}
is a Poisson cluster variety when
G
is of adjoint type.
Relations between the Poisson
groupoid structure defined in this paper and the cluster structure on these varieties defined in
[
25
]

will be a very interesting topic to explore (see
[
25
, Remark 1.10]
).

For the second isomorphic model, consider the open sub-manifold
F_{n}^{o}
of
F_{n}
given by

F_{n}^{o}=\{[g_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{n}}:\;g_{1}g_{2}\cdots g_{n}\in B_{-}B/B\},

and for

{\bf w}\in W^{n}
, let

{\mathcal{O}}^{\bf w}_{e}={\mathcal{O}}^{\bf w}\cap F_{n}^{o}=\{[g_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{n}}\in{\mathcal{O}}^{{\bf w}}:g_{1}g_{2}\cdots g_{n}\in B_{-}B/B\}.

(1.6)

By
[
18
, Theorem 1.1]
,
{\mathcal{O}}^{\bf w}_{e}
is the open
T
-leaf of
({\mathcal{O}}^{\bf w},\pi_{n})
. The Poisson variety

(F_{n}^{o},\pi_{n})
has a natural
T
-extension

(F_{n}^{o}\times T,\pi_{n}\bowtie 0)
, whose
T
-leaves with respect to the
diagonal action of
T
are precisely all the sub-varieties
{\mathcal{O}}^{\bf w}_{e}\times T
for
{\bf w}\in W^{n}
.
Here
\pi_{n}\bowtie 0
, as a Poisson structure on
F_{n}^{o}\times T
, is the
sum of the product Poisson structure
(\pi_{n},0)
and a certain mixed term defined using the
T
-action on
F_{n}^{o}

(see (
1.9
)). We show in Corollary
4.5
that, via a Poisson isomorphism

J_{2n}:(\Gamma_{2n},\widetilde{\pi}_{2n})\to(F_{2n}^{o}\times T,\pi_{2n}\bowtie 0)
and for each
{\bf u}\in W^{n}
,
the Poisson groupoid
(\Gamma^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})},\widetilde{\pi}_{2n})\rightrightarrows({\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u},\pi_{n})
is isomorphic to a Poisson groupoid

({\mathcal{O}}^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})}_{e}\times T,\,\pi_{2n}\bowtie 0)\rightrightarrows({\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u},\pi_{n}).

(1.7)

The advantages of the isomorphic model in (
1.7
) are at least three-fold.
First of all, as

({\mathcal{O}}^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})}_{e}\times T,\pi_{2n}\bowtie 0)
is the
T
-extension of
({\mathcal{O}}^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})}_{e},\pi_{2n})
,
we can apply results in
[
5
,
16
]
on arbitrary generalized Schubert cells and their
T
-extensions.
In particular, the model in (
1.7
) allows us to describe all the symplectic leaves of
(\Gamma^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})},\widetilde{\pi}_{2n})
, thereby proving that
the symplectic leaf of
(\Gamma^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})},\widetilde{\pi}_{2n})
through the section of units of the groupoid
\Gamma^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})}\rightrightarrows{\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u}
is a symplectic groupoid over

({\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u},\pi_{n})
.

Secondly, each
({\mathcal{O}}^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})}_{e}\times T,\pi_{2n}\bowtie 0)
is a (localization of a)
Poisson symmetric CGL extension by
[
5
, Theorem 5.12]
and

[
20
,
\lx@sectionsign
4.2]
, so
the Goodearl-Yakimov theory
in
[
12
]
gives a cluster variety structure on
{\mathcal{O}}^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})}_{e}\times T
. The isomorphisms
{\mathcal{O}}^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})}_{e}\times T\cong\Gamma^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})}\cong{\mathcal{C}}^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})}

thus provide tools for future research on comparing the cluster structure on
{\mathcal{O}}^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})}_{e}\times T
via the Goodearl-Yakimov theory
with that on
{\mathcal{C}}^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})}
established by L. Shen and D. Weng in
[
25
]
.

Thirdly, in their work
[
9
]
on Lagrangian fillings of Legendrian links, H. Gao, L. Shen, and D. Weng show that
varieties of the form
{\mathcal{O}}^{\bf w}_{e}
for
G=SL_{n}
are isomorphic to
augmentation varieties
of certain
positive braid Legendrian links. It would be very interesting to explore connections between the Poisson groupoid structure on
{\mathcal{O}}^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})}_{e}\times T

in this paper and the results in
[
9
]
.

1.4.
The third isomorphic model via generalized double Bruhat cells

For the third isomorphic model for the Poisson groupoid
(\Gamma^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})},\widetilde{\pi}_{2n})\rightrightarrows({\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u},\pi_{n})
, we first explain some background.
Recall from
[
8
]
that
associated to each pair
u,v\in W
one has the double Bruhat cell
G^{u,v}=BuB\cap B_{-}vB_{-}
, and that
(see, for example,
[
13
]
) the decomposition

G=\bigsqcup_{u,v\in W}G^{u,v}
is that of
(G,\pi_{\rm st})
into
T
-leaves for the
T
-action on
G
by left translation.
The first two authors proved in
[
19
]

that for any
u\in W
,
(G^{u,u},\pi_{\rm st})
is a Poisson groupoid over
({\mathcal{O}}^{u},\pi_{1})
, but the groupoid structure
depends on a choice
\dot{u}
of a
representative of
u
in the normalizer subgroup
N_{G}(T)
of
T
in
G
. Using the description of
symplectic leaves of
\pi_{\rm st}
in
G^{u,u}
given by M. Kogan and A. Zelevinsky in
[
13
]
, it is then proved in
[
19
]
that
all symplectic leaves of
(G^{u,u},\pi_{\rm st})
are symplectic groupoids over
({\mathcal{O}}^{u},\pi_{1})
.

Generalizing the decomposition of
G
into double Bruhat cells, the first two authors introduced in
[
18
]

a Poisson manifold
(G_{n,n},\widetilde{\pi}_{n,n})
, for each integer
n\geq 1
, and its decomposition

G_{n,n}=\bigsqcup_{{\bf u},{\bf v}\in W^{n}}G^{{\bf u},{\bf v}}

into
T
-leaves, where each
G^{{\bf u},{\bf v}}
is called a
generalized double Bruhat cell
(one has
(G_{1,1},\pi_{1,1})\cong(G,\pi_{\rm st})
).
The second author then showed in
[
24
]
that
for every
{\bf u}\in W^{n}
,
(G^{{\bf u},{\bf u}},\widetilde{\pi}_{n,n})
is a Poisson groupoid over
({\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u},\pi_{n})
, where again the
groupoid structure depends on a choice
\dot{{\bf u}}=(\dot{u}_{1},\ldots,\dot{u}_{n})\in N_{G}(T)^{n}
representing
{\bf u}
.
The question of
whether or not symplectic leaves of
(G^{{\bf u},{\bf u}},\widetilde{\pi}_{n,n})
are symplectic groupoids over
({\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u},\pi_{n})
was left unanswered in

[
24
]
, due to the fact that description of symplectic leaves of
(G^{{\bf u},{\bf u}},\widetilde{\pi}_{n,n})
was not available.

In this paper, we show that for any
{\bf u}\in W^{n}
, each choice
\dot{{\bf u}}\in N_{G}(T)^{n}
representing
{\bf u}

gives a
T
-equivariant Poisson embedding

E_{\dot{{\bf u}}}:\;(G^{{\bf u},{\bf u}},\,\widetilde{\pi}_{n,n})\;{\hookrightarrow}\;(\Gamma_{2n},\,\widetilde{\pi}_{2n})

(1.8)

whose image is exactly
\Gamma^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})}
.
The Poisson groupoid
(G^{{\bf u},{\bf u}},\widetilde{\pi}_{n,n})\rightrightarrows({\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u},\pi_{n})
defined in
[
24
]
using the choice

\dot{{\bf u}}
is then shown to become precisely the groupoid
(\Gamma^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})},\widetilde{\pi}_{2n})\rightrightarrows({\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u},\pi_{n})
via
E_{\dot{{\bf u}}}
.
Through the (
\dot{{\bf u}}
-dependent) Poisson embedding

E_{\dot{{\bf u}}}
, we have thus an intrinsic explanation on the origin of
the Poisson groupoid structures
on
G^{{\bf u},{\bf u}}
as well as on their dependence on the representatives
\dot{{\bf u}}
.
At this connection, we point out that the construction of the Poisson groupoids
(G^{{\bf u},{\bf u}},\widetilde{\pi}_{n,n})\rightrightarrows({\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u},\pi_{n})

in
[
24
]
is based on a general theory
on local Poisson groupoids over mixed product Poisson manifolds and actions by double symplectic groupoids,
an approach completely different from what we use in this paper.

When
n=1
,

\Gamma_{2}=(G/B)\times B_{-}
is the action groupoid

(G/B)\times B_{-}\rightrightarrows G/B

for the action of
B_{-}
on
G/B
by left translation. The fact that
(\Gamma_{2},\widetilde{\pi}_{2})
is a Poisson groupoid over

(G/B,\pi_{1})
, and that one has the Poisson embeddings in (
1.8
) for
n=1
, are also proved in
[
19
]
.
Putting these
results in
[
19
]
(for
n=1
) and that in
[
24
]
(on the Poisson groupoid structures on

G^{{\bf u},{\bf u}}
) in one unified framework was part of the motivation for this paper.

1.5.
Organization of the paper

After a general construction in
\lx@sectionsign
2
of
a series of Poisson groupoids associated to any Poisson Lie group and a closed Poisson Lie sub-group, we turn to
the Poisson Lie group
(G,\pi_{\rm st})
and its Poisson Lie sub-group
B
in
\lx@sectionsign
3
, where we introduce the
total configuration Poisson groupoids

(\Gamma_{2n},\widetilde{\pi}_{2n})\rightrightarrows(F_{n},\pi_{n}),\hskip 14.45377ptn\geq 1,

of flags. In
\lx@sectionsign
4
, we discuss the Poisson sub-groupoids

(\Gamma^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})},\widetilde{\pi}_{2n})\rightrightarrows({\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u},\pi_{n}),\hskip 14.45377pt{\bf u}\in W^{n},\,n\geq 1,

of
(\Gamma_{2n},\widetilde{\pi}_{2n})\rightrightarrows(F_{n},\pi_{n})
and their three isomorphic models.
In
\lx@sectionsign
5
we prove that the symplectic leaf of
(\Gamma^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})},\widetilde{\pi}_{2n})
passing through the section of units of the groupoid

\Gamma^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})}\rightrightarrows{\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u}
is a symplectic groupoid over
({\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u},\pi_{n})
.

The paper contains several extensive appendices.
The most technical parts of the paper are the proofs that
various isomorphisms between varieties are in fact isomorphisms of Poisson varieties, and we present these proofs in the appendices

\lx@sectionsign
A
-
\lx@sectionsign
C
.

In
\lx@sectionsign
A
, we
show how the various Poisson varieties considered in this paper are
mixed product Poisson varieties
as defined in
[
17
]
.

In
\lx@sectionsign
B
, we show that the
T
-equivariant isomorphism
J_{n}:\Gamma_{n}\to F_{n}^{o}\times T
defined in (
3.14
) is a Poisson
isomorphism from
(\Gamma_{n},\widetilde{\pi}_{n})
to
(F_{n}^{o}\times T,\pi_{n}\bowtie 0)
.

Generalizing the case of
m=n
from
[
18
,
\lx@sectionsign
1.4]
, we introduce in
\lx@sectionsign
C

a
T
-Poisson manifold
(G_{m,n},\widetilde{\pi}_{m,n})
for any pair of integers
m,n\geq 1
whose
T
-leaves are shown to be

generalized double Bruhat cells

G^{{\bf u},{\bf v}}
, where
({\bf u},{\bf v})\in W^{m}\times W^{n}
.
The main results of
\lx@sectionsign
C
are certain explicit
T
-equivariant Poisson isomorphisms between the single
T
-leaves

(G^{{\bf u},{\bf v}},\widetilde{\pi}_{m,n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\left({\mathcal{O}}^{({\bf u},{\bf v}^{-1})}_{e}\times T,\,\pi_{m+n}\bowtie 0\right)

for all
({\bf u},{\bf v})\in W^{m}\times W^{n}
, and similar Poisson isomorphisms between
T
-leaves of

(\widetilde{F}_{n},\widetilde{\pi}_{n})
to those of the form

({\mathcal{O}}^{{\bf w}}_{e}\times T,\pi_{n+1}\bowtie 0)
for
{\bf w}\in W^{n+1}
.
These facts illustrate again the role of generalized Schubert cells (or their
T
-extensions for the examples in this paper) as building blocks for
Poisson varieties associated to the Poisson Lie group
(G,\pi_{\rm st})
.

In
\lx@sectionsign
D
we determine the symplectic leaves of

({\mathcal{O}}^{\bf w}_{e}\times T,\pi_{n}\bowtie 0)
for any
n\geq 1
and
{\bf w}\in W^{n}
. Although only the cases of
{\bf w}=({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})
are needed in the main
text of the paper, the results for arbitrary
{\bf w}
allow us to determine the symplectic leaves in all generalized double Bruhat cells
G^{{\bf u},{\bf v}}
,
thereby extending the result of M. Kogan and A. Zelevinsky in
[
13
]
for the case of
u,v\in W
.
We in fact describe the symplectic leaves in all the three series

(F_{n}^{o}\times T,\,\pi_{n}\bowtie 0),\hskip 14.45377pt(G_{m+n},\,\widetilde{\pi}_{m,n}),\hskip 14.45377pt(\widetilde{F}_{n},\,\widetilde{\pi}_{n}),\hskip 14.45377ptm,n\geq 1,

of
T
-Poisson varieties. Results in
\lx@sectionsign
D

provide test stone examples towards a general theory of symplectic leaves in
T
-leaves to be carried out elsewhere.

1.6.
Notation and basic definitions

For a manifold
X
and
1\leq k\leq\dim X
, let
{\mathfrak{X}}^{k}(X)
be the space of all

k
-vector fields on
X
, i.e., all smooth sections of
\wedge^{k}TX
, where
TX
is the tangent bundle of
X
. When
X
is a complex manifold,

TX
will stand for the holomorphic tangent bundle and
{\mathfrak{X}}^{k}(X)
the space of all holomorphic
k
-vector fields on
X
.
If
X
and
Y
are manifolds and
if
V_{\scriptscriptstyle X}\in{\mathfrak{X}}^{k}(X)
and
V_{\scriptscriptstyle Y}\in{\mathfrak{X}}^{k}(Y)
, let

(V_{\scriptscriptstyle X},0),(0,V_{\scriptscriptstyle Y})\in{\mathfrak{X}}^{k}(X\times Y)
be given by

(V_{\scriptscriptstyle X},\,0)(x,y)=i_{y}V_{\scriptscriptstyle X}(x)\hskip 14.45377pt\mbox{and}\hskip 14.45377pt(0,\,V_{\scriptscriptstyle Y})(x,y)=i_{x}V_{\scriptscriptstyle Y}(y),\hskip 14.45377ptx\in X,y\in Y,

where
i_{y}:X\to X\times Y,\,x^{\prime}\mapsto(x^{\prime},y)

for
x^{\prime}\in X
, and
i_{x}:Y\to X\times Y,\,y^{\prime}\mapsto(x,y^{\prime})
for
y^{\prime}\in Y
. This convention also extends to multi-vector fields on

n
-fold product manifolds
X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{n}
for any integer
n\geq 2
. If
V_{i}\in{\mathfrak{X}}^{k}(X_{i})
for
i\in[1,n]
, we also write

\displaystyle(V_{1},V_{2},\ldots,V_{n})

\displaystyle=V_{1}\times V_{2}\times\cdots\times V_{n}

\displaystyle=(V_{1},0,\ldots,0)+(0,V_{2},\ldots,0)+\cdots+(0,0,\ldots,V_{n})\in{\mathfrak{X}}^{k}(X_{1}\times X_{2}\times\cdots\times X_{n}).

Recall that a Poisson manifold is a pair
(X,\pi)
, where
X
is a manifold and

\pi\in{\mathfrak{X}}^{2}(X)
, called a Poisson structure, satisfies
[\pi,\pi]=0
, where
[\,,\,]
is the
Schouten bracket on
{\mathfrak{X}}^{\bullet}(X)=\oplus_{k}{\mathfrak{X}}^{k}(X)
. For a Poisson structure
\pi
on
X
, define

\pi^{\#}:\;\;T^{*}X\longrightarrow TX,\;\;(\pi^{\#}(\alpha),\;\beta)=\pi(\alpha,\beta),

where
\alpha
and
\beta
are any
1
-forms on
X
. If
X_{1}
is a Poisson sub-manifold of
(X,\pi)
, i.e.,
X_{1}
is a sub-manifold of
X
such that

\pi(x)\in\wedge^{2}T_{x}X_{1}
for all
x\in X_{1}
, the restriction of
\pi
to
X_{1}
will still be denoted by
\pi
, so that
(X_{1},\pi)
is a Poisson manifold.

The identity element of a group is typically denoted as
e
.
Let
A
be any Lie group with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{a}
. The left and right translations on
A
by
a\in A
will be
denoted by
L_{a}
and
R_{a}
respectively, and for any integer
k\geq 1
and

x\in\wedge^{k}\mathfrak{a}
and
\xi\in\wedge^{k}\mathfrak{a}^{*}
,
x^{L}
and
x^{R}
(resp.
\xi^{L}
and
\xi^{R}
)
will respectively
denote the left and right invariant
k
-vector fields (resp.
k
-forms) on
A
with value
x
(resp.
\xi
)
at
e\in A
.
If
A
acts on a manifold
Y
from the right with the action map

\rho:Y\times A\to Y
, we will also denote by
\rho
the Lie algebra homomorphism

\rho:\;\mathfrak{a}\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{X}}^{1}(X),\;\;\rho(x)(y)=\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}\rho(y,\exp(tx)),\hskip 14.45377ptx\in\mathfrak{a},\,y\in Y.

Similarly, if
\lambda:A\times Y\to Y
is a left action by
A
, one has the Lie algebra anti-homomorphism

\lambda:\;\mathfrak{a}\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{X}}^{1}(X),\;\;\lambda(x)(y)=\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}\lambda(\exp(tx),y),\hskip 14.45377ptx\in\mathfrak{a},\,y\in Y.

For clarity and for convenience of the reader, we recall some terminology on groupoids and Poisson groupoids and refer to

[
22
,
27
,
28
]
for the basics of the subject.

Definition 1.1
.

1) A sub-groupoid of a groupoid
\Gamma\rightrightarrows M
is said to be
wide

if it contains the set of units of
\Gamma\rightrightarrows M
.

2) The

full sub-groupoid
of a groupoid
\Gamma\rightrightarrows M
over a subset
M^{\prime}\subset M
is
the intersection
\theta_{+}^{-1}(M^{\prime})\cap\theta_{-}^{-1}(M^{\prime})
as a groupoid over
M^{\prime}
whose structure maps are the restrictions of
those for
\Gamma\rightrightarrows M
, where
\theta_{+}
and
\theta_{-}
are respectively the source and target maps of
\Gamma\rightrightarrows M
.

Definition 1.2
.

A
Poisson groupoid

is a Lie groupoid
\Gamma\rightrightarrows M
together with a Poisson structure
\pi
on
\Gamma
such that

\{(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime},m(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})):\theta_{-}(\gamma)=\theta_{+}(\gamma^{\prime})\}\subset\Gamma^{3}

is a coisotropic sub-manifold of
(\Gamma^{3},\pi\times\pi\times(-\pi))
, where

\theta_{+},\theta_{-}:\Gamma\to M
are the source and target maps, and

m:\{(\gamma,\,\gamma^{\prime})\in\Gamma^{2}:\;\theta_{-}(\gamma)=\theta_{+}(\gamma^{\prime})\}\longrightarrow\Gamma

is the partially defined
multiplication on
\Gamma
. In such a case, there is a unique Poisson structure
\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle M}
on
M
such that
\theta_{+}:(\Gamma,\pi)\to(M,\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle M})

is Poisson and
\theta_{-}:(\Gamma,\pi)\to(M,\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle M})

is anti-Poisson, and we also say that
(\Gamma,\pi)
is a Poisson groupoid over
(M,\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle M})
. If in addition
\pi
is non-degenerate and

\dim\Gamma=2\dim M
, one says that
(\Gamma,\pi)\rightrightarrows(M,\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle M})
is a symplectic groupoid over
(M,\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle M})
.

A Poisson (resp. symplectic) groupoid
(\Gamma,\pi)\rightrightarrows(M,\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle M})
is said to be
(complex) algebraic
if
both
\Gamma
and
M
are smooth algebraic manifolds over
{\mathbb{C}}
, all structure maps of
\Gamma\rightrightarrows M
are smooth algebraic morphisms, and
both
\pi
and
\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle M}
are algebraic Poisson (resp. symplectic) structures.

\diamond

We also recall the notion of
\mathbb{T}
-leaves that will be used throughout the paper.

Definition 1.3
.

If
\mathbb{T}
is a torus, by a
\mathbb{T}
-Poisson manifold we mean a Poisson manifold
(X,{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}})
with an action of

\mathbb{T}
by Poisson isomorphisms. For a
\mathbb{T}
-Poisson manifold
(X,{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}})
, a
\mathbb{T}
-orbit of symplectic leaves
, or
a
\mathbb{T}
-leaf
for short, of
(X,{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}})
is a sub-manifold
L
of
X
of the form

L=\bigcup_{t\in\mathbb{T}}t\Sigma,

where
\Sigma
is a symplectic leaf of
(X,{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}})
, and the map
\mathbb{T}\times\Sigma\to L,(t,x)\to tx,
is a submersion.

We now recall a construction from
[
16
,
\lx@sectionsign
2.6]
.
Let
\mathfrak{t}
be the Lie algebra of
\mathbb{T}
and assume that
\langle\,,\,\rangle
is a symmetric non-degenerate bilinear form on
\mathfrak{t}
. Let
\{h_{i}:i=1,\ldots,r=\dim\mathfrak{t}\}

be an orthonormal basis of
\mathfrak{t}
with respect to
\langle\,,\,\rangle
. Given a
\mathbb{T}
-Poisson manifold
(X,{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}})
with the
\mathbb{T}
-action

\mathbb{T}\times X\to X
, we then have the Poisson structure
{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}}\bowtie_{\sigma}0
on
X\times\mathbb{T}
given by

{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}}\bowtie_{\sigma}0=({\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}},\,0)+\sum_{i=1}^{r}(\sigma(h_{i}),0)\wedge(0,h_{i}^{R}),

(1.9)

where
h_{i}^{R}
denoted the right (left) invariant vector field on
\mathbb{T}
defined by
h_{i}
.
We call
{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}}\bowtie_{\sigma}0
a
\mathbb{T}
-extension of the Poisson structure

{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}}
, and we call

(X\times\mathbb{T},{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}}\bowtie_{\sigma}0)
a
\mathbb{T}
-extension of the
\mathbb{T}
-Poisson manifold

(X,{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}})
.
It is easy to see that
(X\times\mathbb{T},{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}}\bowtie 0)
is a
\mathbb{T}
-Poisson manifold with respect to the diagonal

\mathbb{T}
-action. The following fact is proved in
[
16
, Lemma 2.23]
.

Lemma 1.4
.

With respect to the diagonal
\mathbb{T}
-action on
X\times\mathbb{T}
, the
\mathbb{T}
-leaves of
(X\times\mathbb{T},{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}}\bowtie_{\sigma}0)

are precisely all the sub-manifolds of the form
L\times\mathbb{T}
, where
L
is a
\mathbb{T}
-leaf of
(X,{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}})
.

1.7.
Acknowledgments

We would like to thank L. Shen and D. Weng for explaining to us their work in
[
25
]
.
The research by the first and the third authors in this paper was partially supported by
the RGC of the Hong Kong SAR, China (GRF 17304415 and GRF 17307718), and the paper was largely written
while the second author was a faculty member at the School of Mathematical Sciences of Shanghai Jiaotong University. The third author acknowledges
support from the NSF China (1210010907).

2.
A series of Poisson groupoids associated to Poisson Lie groups

In this section, we give a construction of a series of Poisson groupoids associated to any Poisson Lie group and a closed Poisson Lie subgroup.

2.1.
Poisson Lie group actions and gauge Poisson groupoids

We refer to
[
3
,
6
]
and especially to
[
17
,
\lx@sectionsign
2]
for basic facts and sign conventions
on Poisson Lie groups
and Lie bialgebras.

Recall first that a Poisson Lie group is a pair
(G,{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle G}})
, where
G
is a Lie group and

{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle G}}
a Poisson bi-vector field on
G
which is
multiplicative
in the sense that
the group multiplication
(G\times G,\,{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle G}}\times{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle G}})\rightarrow(G,{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle G}})

is a Poisson map. A right Poisson action of a Poisson Lie group
(G,{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle G}})
on a
Poisson manifold
(X,{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}})
is, by definition, a Poisson map

\rho:\;\;(X\times G,\,{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}}\times{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle G}})\longrightarrow(X,\,{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}}),\;\;(x,\,g)\longmapsto xg,

(2.10)

which also defines a right Lie group action of
G
on
X
. Left Poisson actions of
(G,{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle G}})
are defined similarly.
Recall also that a
coisotropic subgroup
of a Poisson Lie group
(G,{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle G}})
is a
Lie subgroup of
G
which is also a coisotropic sub-manifold with respect to
the Poisson structure
{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle G}}
. The following fundamental fact is proved in
[
26
]
.

Lemma 2.1
.

Suppose that
\rho
is a Poisson Lie group action as in (
2.10
), and suppose that

Q
is a coisotropic subgroup of
(G,{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle G}})
such
that the restricted action of
Q
on
X
is free and the quotient
X/Q
is a smooth manifold. Then the Poisson structure
{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}}
projects to
a well-defined Poisson structure on
X/Q
, which will be called the quotient Poisson structure of
{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}}
.

Example 2.2
.

Suppose that
(X,{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}})
and
(Y,{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle Y}})
are Poisson manifolds,
both with right Poisson actions by a Poisson Lie group
(G,{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle G}})
and such that the diagonal action
of
G
on
X\times Y
is free and the quotient space
(X\times Y)/G
is a smooth manifold. Then
(X\times Y,{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}}\times(-{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle Y}}))
has the right
product Poisson action by the product Poisson Lie group
(G\times G,{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle G}}\times(-{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle G}}))
.
Since the diagonal of
G\times G
is a coisotropic subgroup with respect to
{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle G}}\times(-{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle G}})
, the quotient space

(X\times Y)/G
has the well-defined quotient Poisson structure of
{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}}\times(-{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle Y}})
.

\diamond

Suppose now that
X\to X/G
is a principal
G
-bundle for a Lie group
G
. Let
(X\times X)/G
be the quotient space for the diagonal

G
-action on
X\times X
, and denote elements in
X/G
by
[x]
and in
(X\times X)/G
by
[x_{1},x_{2}]
, where

x,x_{1},x_{2}\in X
. Recall that the
gauge groupoid
of
X\to X/G
is the manifold

(X\times X)/G
with the following Lie groupoid structure
over
X/G
:

\displaystyle\mbox{source map}\;\theta_{+}:\;\;(X\times X)/G\longrightarrow X/G:\;\;[x_{1},\,x_{2}]\longmapsto[x_{1}],

\displaystyle\mbox{target map}\;\theta_{-}:\;\;(X\times X)/G\longrightarrow X/G:\;\;[x_{1},\,x_{2}]\longmapsto[x_{2}],

\displaystyle\mbox{unit map}\;\epsilon:\;\;X/G\longrightarrow(X\times X)/G,\;\;[x]\longmapsto[x,\,x],

\displaystyle\mbox{inverse map}\;{\rm inv}:\;\;(X\times X)/G\longrightarrow(X\times X)/G:\;\;[x_{1},\,x_{2}]\longmapsto[x_{2},x_{1}],

\displaystyle\mbox{multiplication:}\;\mbox{for}\;\gamma=[x_{1},\,x_{2}]\;\mbox{and}\;\gamma^{\prime}=[x_{3},\,x_{4}]\;\mbox{with}\;\theta_{-}(\gamma)=\theta_{+}(\gamma^{\prime}),\;\gamma\gamma^{\prime}=[x_{1}g,\,x_{4}],

\displaystyle\hskip 14.45377pt\hskip 14.45377pt\hskip 14.45377pt\hskip 14.45377pt\hskip 14.45377pt\mbox{where}\;g\in G\;\mbox{is the unique element such that}\;x_{2}g=x_{3}.

Assume, in addition, that
{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle G}}
is a multiplicative Poisson structure on
G
,
{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}}
is a Poisson structure
on
X
such that the
G
-action on
X
for the principal bundle
X\to X/G
is a right Poisson action of the Poisson Lie group
(G,\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle G})

on
(X,{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}})
.
Let
\pi\in{\mathfrak{X}}^{2}((X\times X)/G)
be the quotient Poisson structure of
{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}}\times(-{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}})
and

\pi_{{\scriptscriptstyle X}/{\scriptscriptstyle G}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}^{2}(X/G)
the quotient Poisson structure of
{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}}
.
The following should be well-known but we have not been able to find a reference.

Lemma 2.3
.

With the Poisson structure
\pi
on
(X\times X)/G
and
\pi_{{\scriptscriptstyle X}/{\scriptscriptstyle G}}
on
X/G
, the gauge groupoid

(X\times X)/G\rightrightarrows X/G
is a Poisson groupoid.

Proof.

Let
\Gamma=(X\times X)/G
for notational simplicity.
Let
Y=\{(x_{1},x_{2},x_{2},x_{4},x_{1},x_{4})\in X^{6}\}
. It is clear that

Y
is a coisotropic sub-manifold of
(X^{6},\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}^{(6)})
, where

\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}^{(6)}={\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}}\times(-{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}})\times{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}}\times(-{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}})\times(-{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}})\times{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}^{2}(X^{6}).

Let
Y_{1}=\{(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime},\gamma\gamma^{\prime})\in\Gamma^{3}:\theta_{-}(\gamma)=\theta_{+}(\gamma^{\prime})\}
,
and let

J:\;X^{6}\longrightarrow\Gamma^{3},\;\;(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4},x_{5},x_{6})\longmapsto([x_{1},x_{2}],[x_{3},x_{4}],[x_{5},x_{6}]).

Then

J(Y)\subset Y_{1}
. Conversely, given
y_{1}=(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime},\gamma\gamma^{\prime})\in Y_{1}
, let

\gamma=[x_{1},\,x_{2}]
and

\gamma^{\prime}=[x_{3},\,x_{4}]
for some
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}\in X
such that

[x_{2}]=[x_{3}]
, and let
g
be the unique element in
G
such that
x_{2}g=x_{3}
.
Then
y=(x_{1}g,x_{2}g,x_{3},x_{4},x_{1}g,x_{4})\in Y
and
J(y)=y_{1}
.
This shows that
J(Y)=Y_{1}
. Since
J:(X^{6},\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}^{(6)})\to(\Gamma^{3},\pi\times\pi\times(-\pi))
is Poisson,
it follows from the definition of coisotropic sub-manifolds that
Y_{1}=J(Y)
is coisotropic in

(\Gamma^{3},\pi\times\pi\times(-\pi))
. Note also that both
\theta_{+}
and
\theta_{-}
are surjective submersions.
Thus
(\Gamma,\pi)
is a Poisson groupoid. By the definition of
the Poisson structure
\pi_{{\scriptscriptstyle X}/{\scriptscriptstyle G}}
,
(\Gamma,\pi)
is a Poisson groupoid over
(X/G,\pi_{{\scriptscriptstyle X}/{\scriptscriptstyle G}})
.

Q.E.D.

We now adapt the constructions of quotient Poisson structures in Example
2.2
and the gauge Poisson groupoids in Lemma
2.3
to a setting
suitable for applications in this paper.

Example 2.4
.

[
17
,
\lx@sectionsign
7.1]

Suppose that
(G,{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle G}})
is a Poisson Lie group,
(X,{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}})
is Poisson manifold with a free

right
Poisson action
(x,g)\mapsto xg
by
(G,{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle G}})
, and
(Y,{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle Y}})
is a Poisson manifold with a
left
Poisson action

(g,y)\mapsto gy

by
(G,{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle G}})
. Then one has the right Poisson action of the Poisson Lie group
(G\times G,{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle G}}\times(-{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle G}}))
on

(X\times Y,\,{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}}\times{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle Y}})
given by

(x,y)\cdot(g_{1},g_{2})=(xg_{1},\;g_{2}^{-1}y)
.
Denote by

X\times_{G}Y
the quotient of
X\times Y
by the diagonal
G
-action

(x,\,y)\cdot g=(xg,\,g^{-1}y)
for
x\in X
,
y\in Y
, and
g\in G
.
Assuming that
X\times_{G}Y
is a smooth manifold, it then has the well-defined quotient Poisson structure of
{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}}\times{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle Y}}
.

\diamond

Assume now that
(X,{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}})
is a Poisson manifold with a right Poisson action
(x,g)\to xg
by a Poisson Lie group
(G,{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle G}})
and
assume that
X\to X/G
is a principal bundle. We make the further assumption that
\kappa:X\to X
is an
anti-Poisson involution
with
respect to the Poisson structure
{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}}
. One then has the unique left action
(g,x)\mapsto gx
of
G
on
X
determined by

\kappa(g^{-1}x)=\kappa(x)g,\hskip 14.45377ptx\in X,\,g\in G,

which is a left Poisson action of
(G,{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle G}})
on
(X,{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}})
. Applying the construction in Example
2.4
, one has the quotient space

X\times_{G}X
of
X\times X
by the right
G
-action

(x_{1},\,x_{2})\cdot g=(x_{1}g,\,g^{-1}x_{2}),\hskip 14.45377ptx_{1},x_{2}\in X,\,g\in G,

and the quotient Poisson structure
\pi
on
X\times_{G}X
of
{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}}\times{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}}
. Denote again
elements in
X/G
by
[x]
and in
X\times_{G}X
by
[x_{1},x_{2}]
, where
x,x_{1},x_{2}\in X
, and let
again
\pi_{{\scriptscriptstyle X}/{\scriptscriptstyle G}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}^{2}(X/G)
be the quotient Poisson structure
of
{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle X}}
.

Lemma 2.5
.

With the assumption and notation as above,
(X\times_{G}X,\pi)
is a Poisson groupoid over
(X/G,\pi_{{\scriptscriptstyle X}/{\scriptscriptstyle G}})
with the following
groupoid structure:

\displaystyle\mbox{source map}\;\theta_{+}:\;\;X\times_{G}X\longrightarrow X/G:\;\;[x_{1},\,x_{2}]\longmapsto[x_{1}],

\displaystyle\mbox{target map}\;\theta_{-}:\;\;X\times_{G}X\longrightarrow X/G:\;\;[x_{1},\,x_{2}]\longmapsto[\kappa(x_{2})],

\displaystyle\mbox{unit map}\;\epsilon:\;\;X/G\longrightarrow X\times_{G}X,\;\;[x]\longmapsto[x,\,\kappa(x)],

\displaystyle\mbox{inverse map}\;{\rm inv}:\;\;X\times_{G}X\longrightarrow X\times_{G}X:\;\;[x_{1},\,x_{2}]\longmapsto[\kappa(x_{2}),\,\kappa(x_{1})],

\displaystyle\mbox{multiplication:}\;\mbox{for}\;\gamma=[x_{1},\,x_{2}]\;\mbox{and}\;\gamma^{\prime}=[x_{3},\,x_{4}]\;\mbox{with}\;\theta_{-}(\gamma)=\theta_{+}(\gamma^{\prime}),\;\gamma\gamma^{\prime}=[x_{1}g,\,x_{4}],

\displaystyle\hskip 14.45377pt\hskip 14.45377pt\hskip 14.45377pt\hskip 14.45377pt\hskip 14.45377pt\mbox{where}\;g\in G\;\mbox{is the unique element such that}\;\kappa(x_{2})g=x_{3}.

Proof.

The map
X\times_{G}X\to(X\times X)/G,[x_{1},x_{2}]\to[x_{1},k(x_{2})]
is both an isomorphism of Lie groupoids and an isomorphism of
Poisson manifolds, where
(X\times X)/G
has the gauge Poisson groupoid for the right
Poisson action
(x,g)\to xg
as in Lemma
2.3
.

Q.E.D.

2.2.
A series of Poisson groupoids

Assume that
(G,{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle G}})
is a Poisson Lie group and that
Q
a closed Poisson Lie subgroup. For each integer
n\geq 1
, one then has
the quotient spaces

X_{n}=\overbrace{G\times_{Q}\cdots\times_{Q}G}^{n}\hskip 14.45377pt\mbox{and}\hskip 14.45377ptY_{n}=\overbrace{G\times_{Q}\cdots\times_{Q}G}^{n}/Q.

(2.11)

The manifolds
X_{n}
and
Y_{n}
can be taken as successive quotient spaces as in Example
2.4
. Consequently, one has the
well-defined quotient Poisson structures on
X_{n}
and
Y_{n}
, i.e., well-defined projections of
the
n
-fold product Poisson structure
\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle G}^{n}
on
G^{n}
, which we will denote respectively as
\pi_{{\scriptscriptstyle X}_{n}}
and
\pi_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}_{n}}
.
By the multiplicativity of
{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle G}}
, one has the following Poisson
Lie group actions

\displaystyle(G,{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle G}})\times(X_{n},\,\pi_{{\scriptscriptstyle X}_{n}})\longrightarrow(X_{n},\,\pi_{{\scriptscriptstyle X}_{n}}),\;(g,\,[g_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle X}_{n}})\longmapsto[gg_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle X}_{n}},

(2.12)

\displaystyle(X_{n},\,\pi_{{\scriptscriptstyle X}_{n}})\times(G,{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle G}})\longrightarrow(X_{n},\,\pi_{{\scriptscriptstyle X}_{n}}),\;([g_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle X}_{n}},\,g)\longmapsto[g_{1},\ldots,g_{n-1},g_{n}g]_{{\scriptscriptstyle X}_{n}},

(2.13)

\displaystyle(G,{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle G}})\times(Y_{n},\,\pi_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}_{n}})\longrightarrow(Y_{n},\,\pi_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}_{n}}),\;(g,\,[g_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}_{n}})\longmapsto[gg_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}_{n}}.

(2.14)

As the inverse map on
G
is an anti-Poisson involution for
(G,{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle G}})
, it follows that

{\bf I}_{{\scriptscriptstyle X}_{n}}:\;(X_{n},\,\pi_{{\scriptscriptstyle X}_{n}})\longrightarrow(X_{n},\,\pi_{{\scriptscriptstyle X}_{n}}),\;[g_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle X}_{n}}\longmapsto[g_{n}^{-1},\ldots,g_{2}^{-1},g_{1}^{-1}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle X}_{n}}

is anti-Poisson. As a direct application of Lemma
2.5
by taking
X=X_{n}
with the right Poisson action by the Poisson Lie group

(Q,{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle G}}|_{\scriptscriptstyle Q})
given in (
2.13
) and by taking
\kappa={\bf I}_{{\scriptscriptstyle X}_{n}}
, one has

Theorem 2.6
.

For any Poisson Lie group
(G,{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle G}})
, any closed Poisson Lie group
Q
of
(G,{\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle G}})
, and any positive integer
n
,

(X_{2n},\pi_{{\scriptscriptstyle X}_{2n}})
is a Poisson groupoid over
(Y_{n},\pi_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}_{n}})
with the following groupoid structure:

\displaystyle\mbox{source map}\;\theta_{+}:\;\;X_{2n}\to Y_{n}:\;\;[g_{1},\ldots,g_{2n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle X}_{2n}}\mapsto[g_{1},\ldots,g_{n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}_{n}};

\displaystyle\mbox{target map}\;\theta_{-}:\;\;X_{2n}\to Y_{n}:\;\;[g_{1},\ldots,g_{2n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle X}_{2n}}\mapsto[g_{2n}^{-1},\ldots,g_{n+1}^{-1}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}_{n}};

\displaystyle\mbox{unit map}\;\epsilon:\;\;Y_{n}\to X_{2n},\;\;[g_{1},\ldots,g_{n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}_{n}}\mapsto[g_{1},\ldots,g_{n},\,g_{n}^{-1},\ldots,g_{1}^{-1}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle X}_{2n}};

\displaystyle\mbox{inverse map}\;{\rm inv}:\;\;X_{2n}\to X_{2n}:\;\;[g_{1},\ldots,g_{2n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle X}_{2n}}\mapsto[g_{2n}^{-1},\ldots,g_{1}^{-1}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle X}_{2n}};

\displaystyle\mbox{multiplication:}\;\mbox{for}\;\gamma=[g_{1},\ldots,g_{2n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle X}_{2n}}\;\mbox{and}\;\gamma^{\prime}=[g_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,g_{2n}^{\prime}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle X}_{2n}}\;\mbox{with}\;\theta_{-}(\gamma)=\theta_{+}(\gamma^{\prime}),

\displaystyle\hskip 14.45377pt\hskip 14.45377pt\gamma\gamma^{\prime}=[g_{1},\;\ldots,\;g_{n},\;g_{n+1}\cdots g_{2n}g_{1}^{\prime}\cdots g_{n}^{\prime}g_{n+1}^{\prime},\;g_{n+2}^{\prime},\ldots,g_{2n}^{\prime}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle X}_{2n}}.

3.
The total configuration Poisson groupoids of flags and isomorphic models

We now apply the construction in
\lx@sectionsign
2
to the standard complex semisimple Lie group
(G,\pi_{\rm st})
.

3.1.
The complex semisimple Poisson Lie group
(G,\pi_{\rm st})

Let
G
be a connected complex semi-simple Lie group with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. We recall
the so-called
standard multiplicative Poisson structure
on
G
and refer the readers to

[
3
,
6
,
17
,
18
]
for more detail.

Fix again a pair

(B,B_{-})
of opposite Borel subgroups of
G
and let
T=B\cap B_{-}
, a maximal torus of
G
. Let
N
and
N-
the respective
unipotent radicals of
B
and
B_{-}
. Denote the Lie algebras of

G,B,B_{-},N,N_{-}
and
T
by
\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{b}_{-},\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{h}
respectively.
Fix also a non-degenerate symmetric invariant bi-linear form

\langle\,,\,\rangle_{\mathfrak{g}}
on
\mathfrak{g}
.
Let
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{h}+\sum_{\alpha\in\Delta}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}

be the root decomposition of
\mathfrak{g}
with respect to
\mathfrak{h}
, let
\Delta^{+}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}
be the set of
positive roots with respect to
\mathfrak{b}
, and for each
\alpha\in\Delta^{+}
, let
E_{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
and
E_{-\alpha}\in\mathfrak{g}_{-\alpha}
be such that
\langle E_{\alpha},E_{-\alpha}\rangle_{\mathfrak{g}}=1
. Let
\{h_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\dim\mathfrak{h}}
be a basis of
\mathfrak{h}
which is orthonormal with respect to
the restriction of
\langle\,,\,\rangle_{\mathfrak{g}}
to
\mathfrak{h}
. Then the element

r_{\rm st}=\sum_{i=1}^{\dim\mathfrak{h}}h_{i}\otimes h_{i}+2\sum_{\alpha\in\Delta^{+}}E_{-\alpha}\otimes E_{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{g}

(3.1)

is called the
standard
r
-matrix
on
\mathfrak{g}
. Let

\Lambda_{\rm st}\in\wedge^{2}\mathfrak{g}
be the skew-symmetric part of
r_{\rm st}
, i.e.,

\Lambda_{\rm st}=\sum_{\alpha\in\Delta^{+}}(E_{-\alpha}\wedge E_{\alpha})=\sum_{\alpha\in\Delta^{+}}(E_{-\alpha}\otimes E_{\alpha}-E_{\alpha}\otimes E_{-\alpha}),

and let
\pi_{\rm st}
be the bi-vector field on
G
defined by (see notation in
\lx@sectionsign
1.6
)

\pi_{\rm st}={\Lambda}_{\rm st}^{L}-{\Lambda}_{\rm st}^{R}.

(3.2)

Then
\pi_{\rm st}
is a multiplicative Poisson structure on
G
, and
(G,\pi_{\rm st})
is a
standard semi-simple Poisson
Lie group
.
It is well-known (see, for example,
[
10
]
) that both
B
and
B_{-}

are Poisson Lie subgroups of
(G,\pi_{\rm st})
.
One thus has the Poisson Lie subgroups

(B,\pi_{\rm st})
and
(B_{-},\pi_{\rm st})
of
(G,\pi_{\rm st})
.

3.2.
The total configuration Poisson groupoids of flags

Continuing with the set-up in
\lx@sectionsign
3.1
, we can now apply the constructions in
\lx@sectionsign
2.2
to the Poisson Lie group

(G,\pi_{\rm st})
and its closed Poisson Lie subgroup
B
. In this particular case, as we have already done in
\lx@sectionsign
1.1
, we denote
the Poisson spaces
X_{n}
and
Y_{n}

in (
2.11
) respectively as

\widetilde{F}_{n}=\overbrace{G\times_{B}\cdots\times_{B}G}^{n}\hskip 14.45377pt\mbox{and}\hskip 14.45377ptF_{n}=\overbrace{G\times_{B}\cdots\times_{B}G}^{n}/B,

and we denote the quotient Poisson structures on
\widetilde{F}_{n}
and
F_{n}
respectively as
\widetilde{\pi}_{n}
and
\pi_{n}
.
Note that we have Poisson Lie group actions

\displaystyle\tilde{\lambda}_{n}:\;\;(G,\pi_{\rm st})\times(\widetilde{F}_{n},\widetilde{\pi}_{n})\longrightarrow(\widetilde{F}_{n},\widetilde{\pi}_{n}),\;\;(g,[g_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{F}}_{n}})\longmapsto[gg_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{F}}_{n}},

(3.3)

\displaystyle\lambda_{n}:\;\;(G,\pi_{\rm st})\times(F_{n},\pi_{n})\longrightarrow(F_{n},\pi_{n}),\;\;(g,[g_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{n}})\longmapsto[gg_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{n}}.

(3.4)

Recall that
T=B\cap B_{-}
, a maximal torus of
G
. Since
\pi_{\rm st}|_{T}=0
, the restrictions of the actions to
T
(see
(
1.2
) and (
1.3
)) make both
(\widetilde{F}_{n},\widetilde{\pi}_{n})
and
(F_{n},\pi_{n})
into
T
-Poisson manifolds.
The multiplicativity of
\pi_{\rm st}
also implies that we have the
well-defined Poisson map

\mu_{{\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{F}}_{n}}:\;(\widetilde{F}_{n},\widetilde{\pi}_{n})\longrightarrow(G,\pi_{\rm st}),\;\;[g_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{F}}_{n}}\longmapsto g_{1}g_{2}\cdots g_{n}.

(3.5)

For
{\bf w}=(w_{1},\ldots,w_{n})\in W^{n}
, set

B{\bf w}B=(Bw_{1}B)\times_{B}\cdots\times_{B}(Bw_{n}B)\subset\widetilde{F}_{n},

(3.6)

the image of
(Bw_{1}B)\times\cdots\times(Bw_{n}B)
in
\widetilde{F}_{n}
.
One thus has

\widetilde{F}_{n}=\bigsqcup_{{\bf w}\in W^{m},v\in W}(B{\bf w}B)\cap\mu_{{\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{F}}_{n}}^{-1}(B_{-}vB_{-})\hskip 14.45377pt(\mbox{disjoint union}).

(3.7)

For
w\in W
, let
l(w)
be the length of
w
. For
{\bf w}=(w_{1},\ldots,w_{n})\in W^{n}
, let
l({\bf w})=l(w_{1})+\cdots+l(w_{n})
.
The following description of
T
-leaves of
(\widetilde{F}_{n},\widetilde{\pi}_{n})
is proved in
[
18
, Theorem 1.3]
.

Proposition 3.1
.

1) For any
{\bf w}\in W^{n}
and

v\in W
, the intersection
(B{\bf w}B)\cap\mu_{{\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{F}}_{n}}^{-1}(B_{-}vB_{-})
is a non-empty smooth sub-manifold of
\widetilde{F}_{n}
of dimension
l({\bf w})+l(v)+\dim T
;

2) The decomposition in (
3.7
)
is that of
\widetilde{F}_{n}
into the
T
-leaves of
\widetilde{\pi}_{n}
.

Introduce now

\Gamma_{n}=\{[g_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{F}}_{n}}:\,g_{1}g_{2}\cdots g_{n}\in B_{-}\}=\mu_{{\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{F}}_{n}}^{-1}(B_{-})\subset\widetilde{F}_{n}.

(3.8)

By Proposition
3.1
,
\Gamma_{n}
is a
union of
T
-leaves of
\widetilde{\pi}_{n}
and thus a Poisson sub-manifold of
(\widetilde{F}_{n},\widetilde{\pi}_{n})
.
We now apply Theorem
2.6
to the Poisson Lie group
(G,\pi_{\rm st})
and its
Poisson Lie subgroup
B
.

Theorem 3.2
.

For any integer
n\geq 1
,
(\widetilde{F}_{2n},\widetilde{\pi}_{2n})
is a Poisson groupoid over
(F_{n},\pi_{n})
with the following groupoid structure:

\displaystyle\mbox{source map}\;\theta_{+}:\widetilde{F}_{2n}\to F_{n},\;[g_{1},\ldots,g_{2n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{F}}_{2n}}\mapsto[g_{1},\ldots,g_{n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{n}};

\displaystyle\mbox{target map}\;\theta_{-}:\widetilde{F}_{2n}\to F_{n},\;[g_{1},\ldots,g_{2n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{F}}_{2n}}\mapsto[g_{2n}^{-1},\ldots,g_{n+1}^{-1}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{n}};

\displaystyle\mbox{unit map}\;\epsilon:F_{n}\to\widetilde{F}_{2n},\;[g_{1},\ldots,g_{n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{n}}\mapsto[g_{1},\ldots,g_{n},g_{n}^{-1},\ldots,g_{1}^{-1}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{F}}_{2n}};

\displaystyle\mbox{inverse map}\;{\rm inv}:\widetilde{F}_{2n}\to\widetilde{F}_{2n},\;[g_{1},\ldots,g_{2n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{F}}_{2n}}\mapsto[g_{2n}^{-1},\ldots,g_{1}^{-1}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{F}}_{2n}};

\displaystyle\mbox{multiplication:}\;\mbox{for}\;\gamma=[g_{1},\ldots,g_{2n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{F}}_{2n}}\;\mbox{and}\;\gamma^{\prime}=[g_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,g_{2n}^{\prime}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{F}}_{2n}}\;\mbox{with}\;\theta_{-}(\gamma)=\theta_{+}(\gamma^{\prime}),

\displaystyle\hskip 14.45377pt\hskip 14.45377pt\hskip 14.45377pt\hskip 14.45377pt\hskip 14.45377pt\gamma\gamma^{\prime}=[g_{1},\;\ldots,\;g_{n},\;g_{n+1}\cdots g_{2n}g_{1}^{\prime}\cdots g_{n}^{\prime}g_{n+1}^{\prime},\;g_{n+2}^{\prime},\,\ldots,\,g_{2n}^{\prime}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{F}}_{2n}}.

Furthermore,
\Gamma_{2n}\subset\widetilde{F}_{2n}
is a wide Poisson
sub-groupoid of
(\widetilde{F}_{2n},\widetilde{\pi}_{2n})\rightrightarrows(F_{n},\pi_{n})
.

Proof.

Applying Theorem
2.6
directly to the Poisson Lie group
(G,\pi_{\rm st})
and its
Poisson Lie subgroup
B
, we see that
(\widetilde{F}_{2n},\widetilde{\pi}_{2n})\rightrightarrows(F_{n},\pi_{n})
as described in
Theorem
3.2
is a Poisson groupoid.
One also checks directly from the definitions
of the structure maps of
\widetilde{F}_{2n}\rightrightarrows F_{n}

that
\Gamma_{2n}

is a wide (set-theoretical) sub-groupoid of
\widetilde{F}_{2n}\rightrightarrows F_{n}
(Definition
1.1
).
As
\Gamma_{2n}
is a Poisson sub-manifold of

(\widetilde{F}_{2n},\widetilde{\pi}_{2n})
, it is a Poisson sub-groupoid of
(\widetilde{F}_{2n},\widetilde{\pi}_{2n})\rightrightarrows(F_{n},\pi_{n})
as long as we prove that
\theta_{+}:\widetilde{F}_{2n}\to F_{n}
restricts to a
submersion from
\Gamma_{2n}
to
F_{n}
. To prove this latter statement, we note the isomorphisms

\displaystyle\widetilde{F}_{2n}\longrightarrow(G/B)^{2n-1}\times G,\;\;[g_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{2n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{F}}_{2n}}\longmapsto(g_{1}{}_{\cdot}B,g_{1}g_{2}{}_{\cdot}B,\ldots,g_{1}g_{2}\cdots g_{2n-1}{}_{\cdot}B,g_{1}g_{2}\cdots g_{2n}),

\displaystyle F_{n}\longrightarrow(G/B)^{n},\;\;[g_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{n}}\longmapsto(g_{1}{}_{\cdot}B,g_{1}g_{2}{}_{\cdot}B,\ldots,g_{1}g_{2}\cdots g_{n}{}_{\cdot}B),

under which
\Gamma_{2n}
is mapped to
(G/B)^{2n-1}\times B_{-}
, and
\theta_{+}|_{\Gamma_{2n}}:\Gamma_{2n}\to F_{n}
becomes the projection of

(G/B)^{2n-1}\times B_{-}
from the product
(G/B)^{n}
of the first
n
factors and is thus a submersion.

Q.E.D.

Definition 3.3
.

For
n\geq 1

the Poisson groupoid
(\Gamma_{2n},\widetilde{\pi}_{2n}))\rightrightarrows(F_{n},\pi_{n})
in Theorem
3.2
is called
the
n
th total configuration Poisson groupoid of flags of
G
.

\diamond

In the next
\lx@sectionsign
3.3
and
\lx@sectionsign
3.4
, we introduce two isomorphic models of

(\Gamma_{2n},\widetilde{\pi}_{2n}))\rightrightarrows(F_{n},\pi_{n})
.

3.3.
The Poisson groupoid
({\mathcal{C}}_{2n},\widehat{\pi}_{2n})\rightrightarrows({\mathcal{B}}^{n},\overline{\pi}_{n})

Recall again that
{\mathcal{B}}=G/B
is the (full) flag variety of
G
, and
{\mathcal{A}}=G/N
is
the decorated flag variety of
G
, where
N
is the unipotent radical of
B
. For an integer
n\geq 1
, recall that we have set

{\mathcal{C}}_{n}={\mathcal{B}}^{n-1}\times{\mathcal{A}}^{o},

(3.9)

where
{\mathcal{A}}^{o}=B_{-}N/N
is the open
B_{-}
-orbit in
{\mathcal{A}}=G/N
.
Referring to an element

f\in{\mathcal{B}}
as a
flag
and an element
\hat{f}

in
{\mathcal{A}}
as a
decorated flag
, the space
{\mathcal{C}}_{n}
then consists of all
n
-tuples

(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n-1},\hat{f}_{n})
, where
f_{1},\ldots,f_{n-1}
are flags and
\hat{f}_{n}
is a decorated flag that is
in general position with the flag
f_{-}
represented by
B_{-}
.

The spaces
{\mathcal{C}}_{n}
appeared in
[
25
]
, where the authors consider the special cases when

G=G_{\rm sc}
is simply connected and when
G=G_{\rm ad}
is of adjoint type.
For any pair of words
(b,d)
of length
n
in the simple reflections in
W
(called positive braids in
[
25
]
),
Shen and Weng introduced certain configuration spaces of flags, denoted as

{\rm Conf}^{b}_{d}({\mathcal{A}}_{\rm sc})
and
{\rm Conf}^{b}_{d}({\mathcal{A}}_{\rm ad})
and called

decorated double Bott-Samelson cells
(see
[
25
,
\lx@sectionsign
2.2]
), which can
be embedded in
{\mathcal{C}}_{n}
for
G=G_{\rm sc}
and
G=G_{\rm ad}
respectively
[
25
,
\lx@sectionsign
2.3]

(see Remark
4.3
).
As one of their main results, Shen and Weng prove in
[
25
, Theorem 1.1 and Theorem 1.2]
that
both
{\rm Conf}^{b}_{d}({\mathcal{A}}_{\rm sc})
and
{\rm Conf}^{b}_{d}({\mathcal{A}}_{\rm ad})

are smooth affine varieties; the coordinate rings
{\mathcal{O}}({\rm Conf}^{b}_{d}({\mathcal{A}}_{\rm sc}))
is an upper cluster algebra,

{\mathcal{O}}({\rm Conf}^{b}_{d}({\mathcal{A}}_{\rm ad}))
is an upper cluster Poisson algebra, and the pair
({\rm Conf}^{b}_{d}({\mathcal{A}}_{\rm sc}),{\rm Conf}^{b}_{d}({\mathcal{A}}_{\rm ad}))

form a cluster ensemble for which the Fock-Goncharov cluster duality conjecture holds. See
[
25
]
for detail.

For
n\geq 1
, consider now the isomorphisms

\displaystyle\widehat{\Theta}_{n}:\;\Gamma_{n}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}_{n},\;[g_{1},\cdots,g_{n-1},g_{n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{F}}_{n}}\longmapsto(g_{1}{}_{\cdot}B,\ldots,g_{1}\cdots g_{n-1}{}_{\cdot}B,\,g_{1}\cdots g_{n-1}g_{n}{}_{\cdot}N),

(3.10)

\displaystyle\Theta_{n}:\;F_{n}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{B}}^{n},\;[g_{1},\cdots,g_{n-1},g_{n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{n}}\longmapsto(g_{1}{}_{\cdot}B,\ldots,g_{1}\cdots g_{n-1}{}_{\cdot}B,\,g_{1}\cdots g_{n-1}g_{n}{}_{\cdot}B).

(3.11)

Under the isomorphisms
\widehat{\Theta}_{2n}:\Gamma_{2n}\to{\mathcal{C}}_{2n}
and
\Theta_{n}:F_{n}\to{\mathcal{B}}^{n}
, one checks directly that
the Lie groupoid
\Gamma_{2n}\rightrightarrows F_{n}
in Theorem
3.2
becomes the
following Lie groupoid
{\mathcal{C}}_{2n}\rightrightarrows{\mathcal{B}}^{n}
, where we denote an element in
{\mathcal{A}}^{o}=B_{-}N/N
as
b_{-}{}_{\cdot}N
for a unique

b_{-}\in B_{-}
:

\displaystyle\mbox{source map}\;\theta_{+}:{\mathcal{C}}_{2n}\to{\mathcal{B}}^{n}:(f_{1},\ldots,f_{2n-1},b_{-}{}_{\cdot}N)\mapsto(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n});

\displaystyle\mbox{target map}\;\theta_{-}:{\mathcal{C}}_{2n}\to{\mathcal{B}}^{n}:(f_{1},\ldots,f_{2n-1},b_{-}{}_{\cdot}N)\mapsto(b_{-}^{-1}f_{2n-1},\ldots,b_{-}^{-1}f_{n});

\displaystyle\mbox{unit map}\;\epsilon:{\mathcal{B}}^{n}\to{\mathcal{C}}_{2n},(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n})\to(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n},f_{n-1},\ldots,f_{1},e{}_{\cdot}N);

\displaystyle\mbox{inverse map}\;{\rm inv}:{\mathcal{C}}_{2n}\to{\mathcal{C}}_{2n}:(f_{1},\ldots,f_{2n-1},b_{-}{}_{\cdot}N)\mapsto(b_{-}^{-1}f_{2n-1},\ldots,b_{-}^{-1}f_{2},b_{-}^{-1}f_{1},\,b_{-}^{-1}{}_{\cdot}N);

\displaystyle\mbox{multiplication:}\;\mbox{for}\;\gamma=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{2n-1},b_{-}{}_{\cdot}N)\;\mbox{and}\;\gamma^{\prime}=(f_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,f_{2n-1}^{\prime},b_{-}^{\prime}{}_{\cdot}N)\;\mbox{with}\;\theta_{-}(\gamma)=\theta_{+}(\gamma^{\prime}),

\displaystyle\hskip 14.45377pt\hskip 14.45377pt\hskip 14.45377pt\hskip 14.45377pt\gamma\gamma^{\prime}=(f_{1},\;\ldots,\;f_{n},\;b_{-}f_{n+1}^{\prime},\;\ldots\;b_{-}f_{2n-1}^{\prime},\;b_{-}b_{-}^{\prime}{}_{\cdot}N).

Define Poisson structures
\widehat{\pi}_{n}
on
{\mathcal{C}}_{n}
and
\overline{\pi}_{n}
on
{\mathcal{B}}^{n}
respectively by

\widehat{\pi}_{n}=\widehat{\Theta}_{n}(\widetilde{\pi}_{n})\hskip 14.45377pt\mbox{and}\hskip 14.45377pt\overline{\pi}_{n}=\Theta_{n}(\pi_{n}).

(3.12)

We now have the following direct consequence of Theorem
3.2
.

Theorem 3.4
.

For any positive integer
n
,
({\mathcal{C}}_{2n},\widehat{\pi}_{2n})
, with the groupoid structure as above, is a algebraic Poisson groupoid over
({\mathcal{B}}^{n},\overline{\pi}_{n})
.

For each
n\geq 1
, we call
\widehat{\pi}_{n}
(resp.
\overline{\pi}_{n}
) the
standard Poisson structure
on
{\mathcal{C}}_{n}
(resp.
{\mathcal{B}}^{n}
).
We prove in Proposition
A.6
(see also Corollary
A.7
)
that
({\mathcal{B}}^{n},\overline{\pi}_{n})
is a
mixed product
of
n
copies of the Poisson variety
({\mathcal{B}},\pi_{1})
in the sense defined in

[
17
]
. A similar statement for

({\mathcal{C}}_{n},\widehat{\pi}_{n})
is given in Remark
A.8
.

3.4.
The Poisson groupoid
(F_{2n}^{o}\times T,\pi_{2n}\bowtie 0)\rightrightarrows(F_{n},\pi_{n})

For
n\geq 1
, let again

F_{n}^{o}=\{[g_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{n}}:\;g_{1}g_{2}\cdots g_{n}\in B_{-}B/B\},

(3.13)

an open sub-manifold of
F_{n}
.
Recall that for
g\in B_{-}B
, we write
g=[g]_{-}[g]_{0}[g]_{+}
with
[g]_{-}\in N_{-},[g]_{0}\in T
, and
[g]_{+}\in N
.
Set also
[g]_{\geq 0}=[g]_{0}[g]_{+}
for
g\in B_{-}B
. Define

J_{n}:\;\;\Gamma_{n}\longrightarrow F_{n}^{o}\times T,\;\;J_{n}([g_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{F}}_{n}})=([g_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{n}},\,[g_{1}g_{2}\cdots g_{n}]_{0}).

(3.14)

Recall that
T
acts on
F_{n}
and
\widetilde{F}_{n}
by (
1.2
) and (
1.3
)
respectively. Let
T
act on itself by translation and on
F_{n}^{o}\times T
diagonally.
Then
J_{n}
is
T
-equivariant and that the inverse of
J_{n}
is given by

J_{n}^{-1}([g_{1},\ldots,g_{n-1},g_{n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{n}},t)=[g_{1},\ldots,g_{n-1},g_{n}[g_{1}\cdots g_{n-1}g_{n}]_{\geq 0}^{-1}t]_{{\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{F}}_{n}}.

(3.15)

On the other hand, we have the Poisson structure
\pi_{n}\bowtie 0
on
F_{n}\times T
which is the

T
-extension of
\pi_{n}
with respect to the
T
-action
\lambda_{n}
on
F_{n}
(see
\lx@sectionsign
1.6
and (
3.4
)), namely,

\pi_{n}\bowtie 0:=(\pi_{n},0)+\sum_{i=1}^{\dim\mathfrak{h}}(\lambda_{n}(h_{i}),0)\wedge(0,h_{i}^{R})\in{\mathfrak{X}}^{2}(F_{n}\times T),

(3.16)

where

\{h_{i}\}
is a basis of the Lie algebra

\mathfrak{h}
of
T
orthonormal with respect to
\langle\,,\,\rangle_{\mathfrak{g}}|_{\mathfrak{h}}
, and for
x\in\mathfrak{h}
,

x^{R}
is again the right (and left) invariant vector field on
T
with value
x
at the
identity element.

Theorem 3.5
.

For any
n\geq 1
,
J_{n}:(\Gamma_{n},\,\widetilde{\pi}_{n})\to(F_{n}^{o}\times T,\,\pi_{n}\bowtie 0)
is a
Poisson isomorphism.

Proof.

This is Theorem
B.1
in the
\lx@sectionsign
B
.

Q.E.D.

Using the isomorphism
J_{2n}:\Gamma_{2n}\to F_{2n}^{o}\times T
, we now transfer the groupoid structure on
\Gamma_{2n}
to one on

F_{2n}^{o}\times T
.
The following Lemma
3.6
is straightforward to check.

Lemma 3.6
.

Under the isomorphism
J_{2n}:\Gamma_{2n}\to F_{2n}^{o}\times T
, the Lie groupoid
\Gamma_{2n}\rightrightarrows F_{n}
becomes the following
Lie groupoid
F_{2n}^{o}\times T\rightrightarrows F_{n}
:

\displaystyle\mbox{source map}\;\theta_{+}:\;F_{2n}^{o}\times T\to F_{n},\;([g_{1},\ldots,g_{2n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{2n}},t)\mapsto[g_{1},\ldots,g_{n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{n}};

\displaystyle\mbox{target map}\;\theta_{-}:\;F_{2n}^{o}\times T\to F_{n},\;

\displaystyle\hskip 14.45377pt\hskip 14.45377pt\hskip 14.45377pt\hskip 14.45377pt\hskip 14.45377pt\;\;\;\;([g_{1},\ldots,g_{2n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{2n}},t)\mapsto[t^{-1}[g_{1}g_{2}\cdots g_{2n}]_{\geq 0}g_{2n}^{-1},g_{2n-1}^{-1},\ldots,g_{n+1}^{-1}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{n}};

\displaystyle\mbox{unit map}\;\epsilon:\;F_{n}\to F_{2n}^{o}\times T,\;[g_{1},\ldots,g_{n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{n}}\mapsto([g_{1},\ldots,g_{n},g_{n}^{-1},\ldots,g_{1}^{-1}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{2n}},e);

\displaystyle\mbox{inverse map}\;{\rm inv}:F_{2n}^{o}\times T\to F_{2n}^{o}\times T:\;\;

\displaystyle\hskip 14.45377pt\hskip 14.45377pt\hskip 14.45377pt\hskip 14.45377pt\hskip 14.45377pt\;\;\;\;([g_{1},\ldots,g_{2n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{2n}},t)\mapsto(t^{-1}[g_{1}g_{2}\cdots g_{2n}]_{\geq 0}g_{2n}^{-1},g_{2n-1}^{-1},\ldots,g_{1}^{-1}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{2n}},\;t^{-1});

\displaystyle\mbox{multiplication:}\;\mbox{for}\;\gamma=([g_{1},\ldots,g_{2n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{2n}},t)\;\mbox{and}\;\gamma^{\prime}=([g_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,g_{2n}^{\prime}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{2n}},t^{\prime})\;\mbox{with}\;\theta_{-}(\gamma)=\theta_{+}(\gamma^{\prime}),

\displaystyle\hskip 14.45377pt\hskip 14.45377pt\gamma\gamma^{\prime}=([g_{1},\;\ldots,\;g_{n},\;g_{n+1}\cdots g_{2n}[g_{1}g_{2}\cdots g_{2n}]_{\geq 0}^{-1}g_{1}^{\prime}\cdots g_{n}^{\prime}g_{n+1}^{\prime},\;g_{n+2}^{\prime},\,\ldots,\,g_{2n}^{\prime}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{2n}},\,tt^{\prime}).

By construction, we have now made
(F_{2n}^{o}\times T,\pi_{2n}\bowtie 0)
into a Poisson groupoid over
(F_{n},\pi_{n})
, as stated
in the following companion of Theorem
3.2
and Theorem
3.4
.

Theorem 3.7
.

For any integer
n\geq 1
,
(F_{2n}^{o}\times T,\pi_{2n}\bowtie 0)
with the groupoid structure as above is a Poisson groupoid over
(F_{n},\pi_{n})
.

4.
Special configuration Poisson groupoids of flags and isomorphic models

4.1.
T
-leaves

In view of the three isomorphic models of the total configuration Poisson groupoids given in
\lx@sectionsign
3.2
-

\lx@sectionsign
3.4
,
we now look at
the
T
-leaves in
(\Gamma_{n},\widetilde{\pi}_{n})
,
({\mathcal{C}}_{n},\widetilde{\pi}_{n})
, and
(F_{n}^{o}\times T,\pi_{n}\bowtie 0)
for any integer
n\geq 1
.

Recall again that
T
acts on
F_{n}
and
\widetilde{F}_{n}
via (
1.2
) and (
1.3
) respectively
and on
{\mathcal{C}}_{n}={\mathcal{B}}^{n-1}\times{\mathcal{A}}^{o}
and on
F_{n}^{o}\times T
diagonally.
Recall also the
T
-equivariant Poisson isomorphisms

\widehat{\Theta}_{n}:\;(\Gamma_{n},\widetilde{\pi}_{n})\longrightarrow({\mathcal{C}}_{n},\widehat{\pi}_{n})\hskip 14.45377pt\mbox{and}\hskip 14.45377ptJ_{n}:\;(\Gamma_{n},\widetilde{\pi}_{n})\longrightarrow(F_{n}^{o}\times T,\pi_{n}\bowtie 0),

respectively given in (
3.10
) and (
3.14
).
For
{\bf w}=(w_{1},\ldots,w_{n})\in W^{n}
, introduce

\Gamma^{{\bf w}}=\Gamma_{n}\cap(B{\bf w}B)\subset\Gamma_{n},\hskip 14.45377pt{\mathcal{C}}^{{\bf w}}=\widehat{\Theta}_{n}(\Gamma^{{\bf w}})\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{n},\hskip 14.45377pt\mbox{and}\hskip 14.45377pt{\mathcal{O}}^{{\bf w}}_{e}\times T\subset F_{n}^{o}\times T,

(4.17)

where recall that

B{\bf w}B
is the image of
Bw_{1}B\times\cdots\times Bw_{n}B
in
\widetilde{F}_{n}
and that (see (
1.6
))

{\mathcal{O}}^{\bf w}_{e}=\{[g_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{n}}\in{\mathcal{O}}^{\bf w}:\;g_{1}g_{2}\cdots g_{n}\in B_{-}B/B\}\subset{\mathcal{O}}^{\bf w}.

(4.18)

By the definition of
J_{n}
, one has
J_{n}(\Gamma^{\bf w})={\mathcal{O}}_{e}^{\bf w}\times T
for each
{\bf w}\in W^{n}
.
It follows from the decomposition
\widetilde{F}_{n}=\bigsqcup_{{\bf w}\in W^{n}}B{\bf w}B
that
one has the disjoint unions

\Gamma_{n}=\bigsqcup_{{\bf w}\in W^{n}}\Gamma^{\bf w},\hskip 14.45377pt{\mathcal{C}}_{n}=\bigsqcup_{{\bf w}\in W^{n}}{\mathcal{C}}^{{\bf w}},\hskip 14.45377ptF_{n}^{o}\times T=\bigsqcup_{{\bf w}\in W^{n}}({\mathcal{O}}^{{\bf w}}_{e}\times T).

(4.19)

Proposition 4.1
.

For any integer
n\geq 1
, the decompositions in (
4.19
) are that of the

T
-leaves of
(\Gamma_{n},\widetilde{\pi}_{n})
,
({\mathcal{C}}_{n},\widetilde{\pi}_{n})
, and
(F_{n}^{o}\times T,\pi_{n}\bowtie 0)
. In particular,
for any
{\bf w}\in W^{n}
,

\widehat{\Theta}_{n}:\;(\Gamma^{\bf w},\widetilde{\pi}_{n})\longrightarrow({\mathcal{C}}^{\bf w},\widehat{\pi}_{n})\hskip 14.45377pt\mbox{and}\hskip 14.45377ptJ_{n}:\;(\Gamma^{\bf w},\widetilde{\pi}_{n})\longrightarrow({\mathcal{O}}^{{\bf w}}_{e}\times T,\pi_{n}\bowtie 0)

are
T
-equivariant isomorphisms of single
T
-leaves.

Proof.

The statement for the
T
-leaf decomposition for
(\Gamma_{n},\widetilde{\pi}_{n})
follows Proposition
3.1
. The rest of Proposition
4.1

follows from the fact that both
\widehat{\Theta}_{n}:(\Gamma_{n},\widetilde{\pi}_{n})\rightarrow({\mathcal{C}}_{n},\widehat{\pi}_{n})
and

J_{n}:(\Gamma_{n},\widetilde{\pi}_{n})\rightarrow(F_{n}^{o}\times T,\pi_{n}\bowtie 0)
are

T
-equivariant Poisson isomorphisms. We also note that
by
[
18
, Theorem 1.1]
, each
{\mathcal{O}}^{\bf w}_{e}
is the unique open
T
-leaf of
({\mathcal{O}}^{\bf w},\pi_{n})
, so the fact that the

T
-leaves of
(F_{n}^{o}\times T,\pi_{n}\bowtie 0)
are precisely of the form
{\mathcal{O}}^{\bf w}_{e}\times T
for
{\bf w}\in W^{n}
also follows from Lemma
1.4
.

Q.E.D.

We give a description of
{\mathcal{C}}^{{\bf w}}
.
Let
G_{\rm diag}
be the diagonal of
G\times G
.
Recall that for
(f_{1},f_{2})\in{\mathcal{B}}^{2}
, the Tits distance from
f_{1}
to
f_{2}
is defined to be
the unique

w\in W
such that
(f_{1},f_{2})\in G_{\rm diag}(e_{\cdot}B,\,w_{\cdot}B)
,
and we write
f_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\longrightarrow}}f_{2}
.
In particular,
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
are said to be in general position if
f_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w_{0}}}{{\longrightarrow}}f_{2}
, where
w_{0}
is the longest element in
W
.
Set

f_{0}=e_{\cdot}B\in{\mathcal{B}}\hskip 14.45377pt\mbox{and}\hskip 14.45377ptf_{-}=w_{0}{}_{\cdot}B\in{\mathcal{B}}.

For
f=b_{-}{}_{\cdot}N\in{\mathcal{A}}^{o}
, where
b_{-}\in B_{-}
, let
\bar{f}=b_{-}{}_{\cdot}B\in{\mathcal{B}}
.
The following statement is now clear from the definition of the isomorphism
\widehat{\Theta}:\Gamma_{n}\to{\mathcal{C}}_{n}
.

Lemma 4.2
.

For
{\bf w}=(w_{1},w_{2},\ldots,w_{n})\in W^{n}
, the sub-variety
{\mathcal{C}}^{{\bf w}}
of
{\mathcal{C}}_{n}={\mathcal{B}}^{n-1}\times{\mathcal{A}}^{o}

consists of all
(f_{1},f_{2},\ldots,f_{n-1},f_{n})\in{\mathcal{B}}^{n-1}\times{\mathcal{A}}^{o}
such that

f_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}f_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}f_{2}\longrightarrow\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w_{n}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\bar{f}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w_{0}}}{{\longrightarrow}}f_{-}.

Remark 4.3
.

For
{\bf w}=(w_{1},\ldots,w_{n})\in W^{n}
, choose
any a reduced decomposition
w_{i}=s_{\alpha_{i,1}}\cdots s_{\alpha_{i,l_{i}}}
for each
1\leq i\leq n
, where
l_{i}=l(w_{i})
, and let

b=(s_{\alpha_{1,1}},\,\ldots,\,s_{\alpha_{1,l_{1}}},\,s_{\alpha_{2,1}},\,\ldots,\,s_{\alpha_{2,l_{2}}},\;\ldots,\;s_{\alpha_{n,1}},\,\ldots,\,s_{\alpha_{n,l_{n}}})\in W^{l({\bf w})}.

One then has
{\mathcal{C}}^{\bf w}\cong{\mathcal{C}}^{b}\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{l({\bf w})}
. In the notation of
[
25
]
,

{\mathcal{C}}^{b}={\rm Conf}_{e}^{{b}}({\mathcal{A}})
.

\diamond

4.2.
The Poisson groupoid
(\Gamma^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})},\widetilde{\pi}_{2n})\rightrightarrows({\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u},\pi_{n})
and two isomorphic models

Let now
{\bf u}=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{n})\in W^{n}
and let
{\bf u}^{-1}=(u_{n}^{-1},\ldots,u_{1}^{-1})
.
Consider

\Gamma^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})}=\Gamma_{2n}\cap(B({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})B).

By Proposition
4.1
,
\Gamma^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})}
is a single
T
-leaf of
(\Gamma_{2n},\widetilde{\pi}_{2n})
.
One checks directly from the definitions that
\Gamma^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})}
is the full sub-groupoid (see Definition
1.1
) of

\Gamma_{2n}\rightrightarrows F_{n}
over
{\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u}
, i.e.,

\Gamma^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})}=\theta_{+}^{-1}({\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u})\cap\theta_{-}^{-1}({\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u}),

where
\theta_{+},\theta_{-}:\Gamma_{2n}\to F_{n}
are the source and target maps
of the groupoid
(\Gamma_{2n},\widetilde{\pi}_{2n})\rightrightarrows(F_{n},\pi_{n})
.

Theorem 4.4
.

For any
{\bf u}=(u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{n})\in W^{n}
,
(\Gamma^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})},\widetilde{\pi}_{2n})\rightrightarrows({\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u},\pi_{n})
is a Poisson sub-groupoid of
(\Gamma_{2n},\widetilde{\pi}_{2n})\rightrightarrows(F_{n},\pi_{n})
.

Proof.

Being a
T
-leaf of
(\Gamma_{2n},\widetilde{\pi}_{2n})
,
\Gamma^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})}
is a Poisson sub-manifold of
(\Gamma_{2n},\widetilde{\pi}_{2n})
.
It remains to show that
\theta_{+}|_{\Gamma^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})}}:\Gamma^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})}\to{\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u}
is a surjective submersion.
Since
\Gamma^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})}
contains the image of
{\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u}\subset F_{n}
under the unit map of
\Gamma_{2n}\rightrightarrows F_{n}
,

\theta_{+}|_{\Gamma^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})}}:\Gamma^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})}\to{\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u}
is surjective. Define

\theta:\;\;{\mathcal{O}}^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u},\;\;[g_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{2n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{2n}}\longmapsto[g_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{n}]_{{\scriptscriptstyle F}_{n}}.

Clearly
\theta
is a submersion.
Under the isomorphism
J_{2n}:\Gamma^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})}\to{\mathcal{O}}^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})}_{e}\times T
, the map
\theta_{+}|_{\Gamma^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})}}:\Gamma^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})}\to{\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u}
becomes the projection

\theta_{+}^{\prime}:{\mathcal{O}}^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})}_{e}\times T\to{\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u},(q,t)\to\theta(q)
.
Since
{\mathcal{O}}^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})}_{e}
is open in
{\mathcal{O}}^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})}
,

\theta_{+}^{\prime}
is a submersion. Thus
\theta_{+}|_{\Gamma^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})}}
is a submersion.

Q.E.D.

Recall from (
3.11
) the Poisson isomorphism
\Theta_{n}:(F_{n},\pi_{n})\to({\mathcal{B}}^{n},\overline{\pi}_{n})
. Set

{\mathcal{B}}^{\bf u}=\Theta_{n}({\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u})=\{(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n})\in{\mathcal{B}}^{n}:f_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}f_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}f_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u_{3}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u_{n}}}{{\longrightarrow}}f_{n}\},

so
{\mathcal{B}}^{\bf u}
is a Poisson sub-manifold of
({\mathcal{B}}^{n},\overline{\pi}_{n})
. We have the following immediate consequence of Theorem
4.4
.

Corollary 4.5
.

For any
n\geq 1
and
{\bf u}\in W^{n}
,
({\mathcal{C}}^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})},\widehat{\pi}_{2n})\rightrightarrows({\mathcal{B}}^{\bf u},\overline{\pi}_{n})
is a Poisson sub-groupoid
of
({\mathcal{C}}_{2n},\widehat{\pi}_{2n})\rightrightarrows({\mathcal{B}}^{n},\overline{\pi}_{n})
in
Theorem
3.4
and is isomorphic, via the isomorphisms
\widehat{\Theta}_{2n}:\Gamma^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})}\to{\mathcal{C}}^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})}
and
\Theta_{n}:{\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u}\to{\mathcal{B}}^{\bf u}
, to the Poisson groupoid

(\Gamma^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})},\widetilde{\pi}_{2n})\rightrightarrows({\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u},\pi_{n})
.

Corollary 4.6
.

For any
n\geq 1
and
{\bf u}\in W^{n}
,

({\mathcal{O}}^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})}_{e}\times T,\,\pi_{2n}\bowtie 0)\rightrightarrows({\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u},\pi_{n})
is a Poisson sub-groupoid of

(F^{o}_{2n}\times T,\pi_{2n}\bowtie 0)\rightrightarrows(F^{n},\pi_{n})
in
Theorem
3.7
and is isomorphic, via the isomorphism
J_{2n}:\Gamma^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{e}^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})}
, to the Poisson groupoid

(\Gamma^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})},\widetilde{\pi}_{2n})\rightrightarrows({\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u},\pi_{n})
.

Definition 4.7
.

For
{\bf u}\in W^{n}
, we refer to either of the three isomorphic Poisson groupoids

(\Gamma^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})},\widetilde{\pi}_{2n})\rightrightarrows({\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u},\pi_{n}),\;\;\;\;({\mathcal{C}}^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})},\widehat{\pi}_{2n})\rightrightarrows({\mathcal{B}}^{\bf u},\overline{\pi}_{n}),\;\;\;\;({\mathcal{O}}^{({\bf u},{\bf u}^{-1})}_{e}\times T,\,\pi_{2n}\bowtie 0)\rightrightarrows({\mathcal{O}}^{\bf u},\pi_{n})

as a
special configuration Poisson groupoid
(of flags of
G
).

\diamond

The special configuration Poisson groupoids have a simple set-theoretical description. We first set up some notation.
Recall that
N_{G}(T)\subset G
is the normalizer of
T
in
G
.
For
u\in W
, let
uT\subset N_{G}(T)
be the set of representatives of
u
in
N_{G}(T)
.
For
\dot{u}\in uT
, set

C_{\dot{u}}=N\dot{u}\cap\dot{u}N_{-}

(4.20)

It is well-known (see, for example,
[
8
, Proposition 2.9]
) that the maps

C_{\dot{u}}\times B\longrightarrow BuB,\,(c,b)\longmapsto cb\hskip 14.45377pt\mbox{and}\hskip 14.45377ptB\times C_{\dot{u}}\longrightarrow Bu^{-1}B,\,(b,c)\longmapsto bc^{-1},

(4.21)

are both isomorphisms.
For
{\bf u}=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{n})\in W^{n}
, let
{\bf u}T^{n}=u_{1}T\times\cdots\times u_{n}T\subset N_{G}(T)^{n}
and call any

\dot{{\bf u}}=(\dot{u}_{1},\ldots,\dot{u}_{n})\in{\bf u}T^{n}
a representative of
{\bf u}
. For
